<hgroup><h1>Unveiling the Potent Antiviral and Antioxidant Activities of an Aqueous Extract from <em>Caesalpinia mimosoides</em> Lamk: Cheminformatics and Molecular Docking Approaches</h1></hgroup><div class="cg p">
<a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Klamrak%20A"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id1"><span class="name western">Anuwatchakij Klamrak</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id1">
<h3><span class="name western">Anuwatchakij Klamrak</span></h3>
<div class="p">
<sup>1</sup>Division of Pharmacognosy and Toxicology, Faculty of Pharmaceutical Sciences, Khon Kaen University, Khon Kaen 40002, Thailand; anuwat_kla@yahoo.com (A.K.); yutthakan_s@kkumail.com (Y.S.); j.piyapon@kkumail.com (P.J.); napapuch.aom25@gmail.com (N.N.); shahinanft@gmail.com (S.S.R.); aoimacth@me.com (T.D.); nnatsa@kku.ac.th (N.N.)</div>
<div class="p">
<sup>2</sup>Protein and Proteomics Research Center for Commercial and Industrial Purposes (ProCCI), Khon Kaen University, Khon Kaen 40000, Thailand; pornsma@kku.ac.th (P.M.); nisaja@kku.ac.th (N.J.); narin@kku.ac.th (R.P.)</div>
<div>Conceptualization, Methodology, Formal analysis, Data curation, Writing – original draft, Writing – review &amp; editing</div>
<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Klamrak%20A"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Anuwatchakij Klamrak</span></a>

<sup>1,</sup><sup>2</sup>, <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Nabnueangsap%20J"[Author]' class="usa-link" aria-describedby="id2"><span class="name western">Jaran Nabnueangsap</span></a><div hidden="hidden" id="id2">
<h3><span class="name western">Jaran Nabnueangsap</span></h3>
<div class="p">

<div class="p">Find articles by <a href='https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/?term="Nabnueangsap%20J"[Author]' class="usa-link"><span class="name western">Jaran Nabnueangsap</span></a>

</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>Our group previously demonstrated that <em>Caesalpinia mimosoides</em> Lamk exhibits many profound biological properties, including anticancer, antibacterial, and antioxidant activities. However, its antiviral activity has not yet been investigated. Here, the aqueous extract of <em>C. mimosoides</em> was prepared from the aerial parts (leaves, stalks, and trunks) to see whether it exerts anti-influenza (H1N1) effects and to reduce the organic solvents consumed during extraction, making it a desirable approach for the large-scale production for medical uses. Our plant extract was quantified to contain 7 g of gallic acid (GA) per 100 g of a dry sample, as determined using HPLC analysis. It also exerts potent antioxidant activities comparable to those of authentic GA. According to untargeted metabolomics (UPLC-ESI(-)-QTOF-MS/MS) with the aid of cheminformatics tools (MetFrag (version 2.1), SIRIUS (version 5.8.3), CSI:FingerID (version 4.8), and CANOPUS), the major metabolite was best annotated as “gallic acid”, phenolics (e.g., quinic acid, shikimic acid, and protocatechuic acid), sugar derivatives, and dicarboxylic acids were deduced from this plant species for the first time. The aqueous plant extract efficiently inhibited an influenza A (H1N1) virus infection of MDCK cells with an IC<sub>50</sub> of 5.14 µg/mL. Of equal importance, hemolytic activity was absent for this plant extract, signifying its applicability as a safe antiviral agent. Molecular docking suggested that GA interacts with conserved residues (e.g., Arg152 and Asp151) located in the catalytic inner shell of the viral neuraminidase (NA), sharing the same pocket as those of anti-neuraminidase drugs, such as laninamivir and oseltamivir. Additionally, other metabolites were also found to potentially interact with the active site and the hydrophobic 430-cavity of the viral surface protein, suggesting a possibly synergistic effect of various phytochemicals. Therefore, the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract may be a good candidate for coping with increasing influenza virus resistance to existing antivirals.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> <em>Caesalpinia mimosoides</em> Lamk, aqueous extract, gallic acid, antioxidant, antivirus, cheminformatics, molecular docking</p></section></section><section id="sec1-foods-13-00081"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Medicinal plants are potential sources of bioactive ingredients used for various purposes, including the treatment of infectious and non-infectious diseases [<a href="#B1-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-foods-13-00081">1</a>,<a href="#B2-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-foods-13-00081">2</a>,<a href="#B3-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-foods-13-00081">3</a>]. Natural plant extracts may provide healing efficacy and patient safety; however, the search for alternative phytochemical constituents with antiviral activity is still relatively limited compared to the search for antibacterial agents [<a href="#B1-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-foods-13-00081">1</a>,<a href="#B3-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-foods-13-00081">3</a>,<a href="#B4-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-foods-13-00081">4</a>]. Thankfully, numerous studies have demonstrated the potential of aqueous and/or ethanolic extracts of plants as promising sources of antiviral agents [<a href="#B5-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-foods-13-00081">5</a>,<a href="#B6-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-foods-13-00081">6</a>,<a href="#B7-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-foods-13-00081">7</a>,<a href="#B8-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-foods-13-00081">8</a>]. Most of them are polyphenols, including hydroxybenzoic acids (e.g., gallic acid (GA) (1) and vanillic acid), hydroxycinnamic acids (e.g., caffeic acid, ferulic acid, and <em>p</em>-coumaric acid), and phenylpropanoid pathway-derived products (e.g., quercetin, kaempferol, cyanidin, resveratrol, and catechin) [<a href="#B9-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-foods-13-00081">9</a>,<a href="#B10-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-foods-13-00081">10</a>,<a href="#B11-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-foods-13-00081">11</a>]. Antiviral activity can be detected by inhibiting viral infection during steps such as attachment, entry, replication, and particle maturation [<a href="#B12-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-foods-13-00081">12</a>]. A recent study showed that the aqueous extract of <em>Toona sinensis</em>, containing catechin and GA (1) as major phenolic compounds, exerts potent antiviral activity against the influenza (H1N1) virus by blocking viral attachment to A549 cells [<a href="#B12-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-foods-13-00081">12</a>,<a href="#B13-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-foods-13-00081">13</a>], specifically by interacting with the viral surface proteins neuraminidase (NA) and hemagglutinin (HA). Ellagic acid (2), the dimeric structure of GA (1), present as a major compound in the aqueous extract of <em>Lagerstroemia speciosa</em>, was able to inhibit replication of human rhinovirus-4 (HRV-4) in HeLa cells [<a href="#B14-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-foods-13-00081">14</a>]. Polyphenols derived from <em>Aronia melanocarpa</em>, such as isoquercetin, kaempferol, ferulic acid, caffeic acid, ellagic acid, and myricetin, have also been shown to have promising anti-influenza activities, with the latter two examples improving the survival rate of mice infected with the rPR8-GFP virus [<a href="#B15-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-foods-13-00081">15</a>,<a href="#B16-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-foods-13-00081">16</a>,<a href="#B17-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-foods-13-00081">17</a>]. Although polyphenols are widely distributed in various plant families, the plant origin and the technological approaches restrict the large-scale production for antiviral applications, including recovery, extraction, and purification [<a href="#B18-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-foods-13-00081">18</a>,<a href="#B19-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-foods-13-00081">19</a>,<a href="#B20-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-foods-13-00081">20</a>]. Therefore, identifying the plants with high levels and varieties of the aforementioned substances (e.g., GA and other derivatives) is of primary importance.</p>
<p><em>Caesalpinia mimosoides</em> Lamk is an edible plant belonging to the <em>Fabaceae</em> family and is known by a variety of local Thai names such as cha rueat (general), thanao song, phak puya, nam puya, phakkat ya, phak khaya, and phak khaya (<a href="http://www.qsbg.org/Database/plantdb/mdp/medicinal-specimen.asp?id=352" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://www.qsbg.org/Database/plantdb/mdp/medicinal-specimen.asp?id=352</a>, accessed on 31 January 2023). In Asian countries, its young shoots and leaves are consumed as fresh or cooked vegetables [<a href="#B21-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-foods-13-00081">21</a>,<a href="#B22-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-foods-13-00081">22</a>]. Historically, this plant has been utilized as a carminative and to heal dizziness and fainting, indicating its potential safety and capacity as a medicinal agent. <em>C. mimosoides</em> Lamk exhibits promising biological activities, including anticancer, antibacterial, antioxidant, anti-inflammatory, and neuroprotective effects [<a href="#B23-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-foods-13-00081">23</a>,<a href="#B24-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-foods-13-00081">24</a>,<a href="#B25-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-foods-13-00081">25</a>,<a href="#B26-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-foods-13-00081">26</a>,<a href="#B27-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-foods-13-00081">27</a>,<a href="#B28-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-foods-13-00081">28</a>,<a href="#B29-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-foods-13-00081">29</a>]. While the virucidal effects of this plant species have not yet been investigated, previous studies revealed that <em>C. minax</em> and <em>C. latisiliqua</em>, which belong to the same genus, were rich in flavonoids, stilbene, phenolics, and other bioactive constituents and demonstrated promising antiviral activity [<a href="#B30-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-foods-13-00081">30</a>,<a href="#B31-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-foods-13-00081">31</a>]. Remarkably, our group demonstrated that the <em>n</em>-hexane extract of this plant possesses anticancer properties against cervical carcinoma cell lines, but the active constituents were still unclear [<a href="#B27-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-foods-13-00081">27</a>]. After partial purification using column chromatography, the most active fraction (F21) consisted of GA (1) (68.49%), ferulic acid (20.63%), vanillic acid (5.59%), caffein (3.44%), and resveratrol (1.85%). These phenolic substances, especially GA (1) as the major constituent, were speculated to play a significant role in the cytotoxic effects on cancer cells. Later, GA (1) was successfully isolated and purified; it was found capable of inhibiting the proliferation of cancer and pathogenic bacterial cells [<a href="#B32-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-foods-13-00081">32</a>]. Although <em>C. mimosoides</em> contains several polyphenols corresponding to antiviral compounds reported in the literature, its antiviral activity has not yet been documented. While phenolics can be extracted from this plant species, the procedures are rather complicated and costly, as large amounts of organic solvents (e.g., <em>n</em>-hexane, MeOH, and EtOAc) are required. Hence, there is great interest in preparing <em>C. mimosoides</em> phenolic-rich extracts with antiviral activity using a green chemistry approach, enabling large-scale production to serve pharmaceutical use.</p>
<p>Apart from NMR, high-resolution LC-MS/MS is one of the most important tools in metabolomics analysis through its remarkable sensitivity and capacity to identify the broadest range of metabolites present in biological samples via untargeted metabolomics analysis [<a href="#B33-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-foods-13-00081">33</a>,<a href="#B34-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-foods-13-00081">34</a>,<a href="#B35-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-foods-13-00081">35</a>,<a href="#B36-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-foods-13-00081">36</a>]. Several tools are now designed for the structural annotation of natural products based on experimental mass data [<a href="#B37-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-foods-13-00081">37</a>,<a href="#B38-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-foods-13-00081">38</a>,<a href="#B39-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-foods-13-00081">39</a>,<a href="#B40-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-foods-13-00081">40</a>]. SIRIUS is a mass spectra prediction tool developed to provide the correct molecular formula and fragmentation pathway of the query subject using high-resolution isotopic pattern analysis [<a href="#B38-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-foods-13-00081">38</a>]. This software also integrates with other tools supporting structure elucidation, including the CSI:FingerID web service and CANOPUS (class assignment and ontology prediction using mass spectrometry), enabling the elucidation of possible candidate structures, substructures, and classifications of the query metabolites [<a href="#B37-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-foods-13-00081">37</a>,<a href="#B39-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-foods-13-00081">39</a>]. For example, Wang et al. [<a href="#B41-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-foods-13-00081">41</a>] recently published structural information on phytochemicals in <em>Salvia miltiorrhiza</em> Bunge, which is utilized in traditional Chinese medicine for treating neurological and cardiovascular diseases, using SIRIUS (version 4) in conjunction with mass spectrometry fragmentation rules. A total of 34 tanshinones, 31 phenolics, and 2 new salvianolic acids (salvianolic acid C and 7″,8″-Dedihydro-salvianolic acid B) were deduced from their untargeted metabolomics data for the first time [<a href="#B42-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-foods-13-00081">42</a>]. Similarly, SIRIUS 4 contributed to the structural annotation of 7 unknown and 17 known bioactive constituents present in the crude extract of <em>Urtica dioica</em> leaves [<a href="#B43-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-foods-13-00081">43</a>]. Thus, knowing the structural details of various compounds found in the aqueous extract of <em>C. mimosoides</em> annotated using the cheminformatics approaches would be extremely helpful for explaining mechanisms underlying parts of its radical scavenging and antiviral activities.</p>
<p>Here, an aqueous extract of <em>C. mimosoides</em> was established from its aerial parts, comprising leaves, stalks, and trunks, gathered from an open field in Khon Kaen province, Thailand. After an assessment of total phenolic content (TPC) and antioxidant properties, HPLC analyses were used to determine whether GA (1) was the major constituent in the extract. Untargeted metabolomics analysis (UPLC-ESI(-)-QTOF-MS/MS) was carried out to gain a better understanding of the phytochemicals in the extract, and structural information was then annotated in the coherent assessment of cheminformatics tools, including MetFrag, SIRIUS, CSI:FingerID, and CANOPUS. The anti-influenza (H1N1) activity of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> extract was evaluated, while molecular docking was employed to elucidate the potential mechanisms responsible for its antiviral activity. The infographic describing an overview of the experiments is shown in <a href="#foods-13-00081-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a> below.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f001"><h3 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h3>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/f340bbcb7925/foods-13-00081-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="499" width="714" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/foods-13-00081-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>An infographic illustrating sample preparation, chemical characterization, cheminformatics-based structure annotation, antiviral assay, and molecular docking of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract. Structural annotation and docking of putative quinic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 191.0607 [M-H]<sup>−</sup>) and 3-Methoxybenzoic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 151.0402 [M-H]<sup>−</sup>) were chosen as the examples in the workflow diagram.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2-foods-13-00081"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Plant Collection and Preparation of C. mimosoides Aqueous Extract</h3>
<p>Fresh aerial <em>C. mimosoides</em> parts, including leaves, stalks, and trunks, were collected from an open field at Nam Phong subdistrict, Khon Kaen province, Thailand, which is located at 16°37′56.7″ north latitude, and 102°46′14.4″ east longitude. Collected plant materials were washed in running water (30–45 min), soaked in tap water (60 min), and finally rinsed with deionized water to remove unwanted residues. Cleaned plant samples were dried at 50 °C for 2–3 days or until constant weight was reached using an oven incubator (BINDER Inc., Bohemia, NY, USA). Oven-dried plant materials were cut into smaller sizes and ground in a blender. To prepare the aqueous extract, dried finely ground plant material (20 g) was extracted with 200 mL of deionized water with shaking at 180 rpm, 25 °C for 2 days in an incubator shaker (N-BIOTEK, Gyeonggi-do, Republic of Korea). The clear supernatant was collected via centrifugation at 25 °C, 8000 rpm for 20 min, decanted into a round-bottom flask, and rotated in a cryo-bath (CoolSafe, Lynge, Denmark) at −80 °C for 1 h. Samples were then freeze-dried at −110 °C (CoolSafe, Denmark) and collected after 48 h. The freeze-dried extract was kept at −80 °C until used.</p></section><section id="sec2dot2-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Total Phenolic Content (TPC)</h3>
<p>The total phenolic content of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract was determined following Folin and Ciocalteu [<a href="#B44-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-foods-13-00081">44</a>] with minor modifications [<a href="#B45-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-foods-13-00081">45</a>]. In brief, the reaction was assessed in a 96-well plate, where an aliquot (20 µL) of extract, purified GA (1) (&gt;95% purity Sigma-Aldrich, St. Louis, MO, USA) or blank (deionized water) was loaded in each well. Then, 100 μL of a 0.2 M Folin–Ciocalteu reagent and 80 μL of 7% (<em>w</em>/<em>v</em>) sodium carbonate were added. Mixtures were incubated at ambient temperature under constant darkness for 30 min. The absorbance value was measured at 760 nm using a microplate reader (Ensight<sup>®</sup> Multimode Plate Reader, PerkinElmer, MA, USA). All detected phenolics were represented as GA equivalents (GAE)/mg dry weight.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Antioxidant Assays</h3>
<p>The 2,2-diphenyl-1-picrylhydrazyl (DPPH) and 2,2′-azinobis-3-ethylbenzotiazoline-6-sulfonic acid (ABTS) assays were used to investigate the radical scavenging activity of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> water extract as recently described by Xiao et al. [<a href="#B46-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-foods-13-00081">46</a>] with some modifications [<a href="#B46-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-foods-13-00081">46</a>]. Purified GA was used as the reference standard, and deionized water was the blank throughout the study.</p>
<p>For the DPPH assay, various concentrations of the extract were mixed with 100 μL of a 0.2 mM DPPH reagent and incubated for 30 min under darkness. The reducing power of phytochemicals was evaluated by monitoring the decreased absorbance at 517 nm using a microplate reader (Ensight<sup>®</sup> Multimode Plate Reader). Results were expressed as IC<sub>50</sub> or the percentage of DPPH radical scavenging activity, which was calculated as follows: inhibition ratio (%) = (A<sub>control</sub> − A<sub>sample</sub>) × 100/A<sub>control</sub>, where A<sub>control</sub> = absorbance of the reaction with deionized water and A<sub>sample</sub> = absorbance of the reaction with extract solution.</p>
<p>For the ABTS assay, 200 μL of an ABTS working solution was mixed with 10 μL of different concentrations of the extract, incubated at room temperature, and protected from light for 7 min. Decolorization was measured at 734 nm using a microplate reader (Ensight<sup>®</sup> Multimode Plate Reader). Results were expressed as IC<sub>50</sub> and/or the percentage of radical scavenging activity, which was calculated according to the formula: % free radical scavenging = (A<sub>control</sub> − A<sub>sample</sub>) × 100/A<sub>control</sub>, where A<sub>control</sub> = absorbance of the reaction with deionized water and A<sub>sample</sub> = absorbance of the reaction with extract solution.</p></section><section id="sec2dot4-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. HPLC Analysis of GA (1) in the C. mimosoides Aqueous Extract</h3>
<p>Reversed-phase high-performance liquid chromatography with a diode array detector (RP-HPLC-DAD) was used to estimate the amount of GA (1) present in the aqueous extract, as previously established by Palasap et al. [<a href="#B27-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-foods-13-00081">27</a>], with slight modifications. The analysis was conducted using HPLC (1260 Infinity II LC System, Agilent, CA, USA) coupled with a PDA detector. A C-18 column (100 mm × 4.6 mm, 5 µm particle size, Phenomenex, CA, USA) was employed. The mobile phases consisted of 0.5% phosphoric acid (solvent A) and methanol (solvent B). Separation was achieved using a linear gradient of solvent B as follows: 5% for 0–2 min, 5–95% for 15 min, 95% for 3 min, and back to 5% for 10 min, through a total run time of 20 min with a flow rate at 0.8 mL/min. The sample temperature was controlled to 25 °C. Column oven temperature was controlled at 25 °C, and chromatograms were recorded at 270 nm. The sample (final concentration of 1 mg/mL) was prepared by reconstituting 10 mg of <em>C. mimosoides</em> powder extract in 10 mL deionized water, centrifuging at the maximum speed (18,894× <em>g</em>, 25 °C, 10 min), and filtering through a polyvinylidene difluoride membrane (Agela Technologies, Wilmington, DE, USA). Purified GA in deionized water (1 mg/mL) was used to generate linear 6-point calibration curves (15.625–500 μg/mL) of the standard. The experiment was run in triplicate.</p></section><section id="sec2dot5-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Detection of Metabolites Using LC-MS/MS</h3>
<p>A non-targeted metabolomic (LC-MS/MS) approach was employed to gain more details concerning the phytochemicals in the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract. Parameters were based on Wu et al. [<a href="#B34-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-foods-13-00081">34</a>], with appropriate modifications [<a href="#B27-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-foods-13-00081">27</a>]. The analysis was implemented using the ultrahigh-performance liquid chromatograph (UHPLC) (UltiMate 3000 RSLCnano UHPLC System, Thermo Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA), which was equipped with an Acclaim<sup>®</sup> RSLC120 C18 (100 × 2.1 mm, 2.2 µm 120 Å, Thermo Scientific, USA) column. The mobile phases consisted of 0.1% formic acid (solvent A) and 0.1% formic acid in acetonitrile (solvent B). Elution was performed at 0.3 mL/min using a linear gradient of the solvent B as follows: 0% for 0–1 min, 0–25% for 1–3 min, 95% for 3–4.5 min, and back to 0% for 2.5 min, for a total run time of 7 min. The one μL of 10 ppm <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract was passed through the column at a temperature of 30 °C. Metabolite identification was confirmed using a mass spectrometer (TripleTOF6600+, AB SCIEX™, Framingham, MA, USA). ESI source conditions were set as follows: the ion source gas 1 50, ion source gas 2 60, curtain gas 30, a temperature of 500 °C, with the ion spray voltage floating of −4500 V. A TOF MS scan range was 100–500 amu., where the product ion scan range was 40–200 Da. The scan accumulation time was 0.1 s, while the parent ion scan accumulation time was 0.25. Mass spectra were generated using a decluttering potential (DP) of −80 V via the collision energy of −30 ± 10 eV. Then, tentative identification of target metabolites was confirmed by comparing their experimental MS/MS spectra with NIST 2017 and Natural Products HR-MS/MS library containing ~13,800 and 1000 substances.</p></section><section id="sec2dot6-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Structural Annotation Using MetFrag Webservice</h3>
<p>Raw data of twenty-four metabolites detected from the extract were further annotated using the MetFrag web service. Structural annotation of the target metabolite could be accomplished via two steps of data processing, “retrieving candidates” and “fragmentation setting &amp; processing” (accessed on 3 July 2023 at <a href="https://ipb-halle.github.io/MetFrag/projects/metfragweb/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://ipb-halle.github.io/MetFrag/projects/metfragweb/</a>). Using the putative mass peak of GA (1) (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.0241 [M-H]<sup>−</sup>) as an example, by searching against the KEGG database, the neutral mass of 170.02153 (relative mass deviation 5 ppm) and its theoretical molecular ion with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.014247 were defined as parameters to receive various candidates of the query subjects. In this step, the neutral formula could also be guided. After receiving a number of candidates, other parameters, including relative mass deviation (5 ppm), absolute mass deviation (0.001), adduct type ([M-H]<sup>−</sup>), and MS/MS peak list (<a href="#app1-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link">Table S1</a>), were then provided in the fragmentation processing step to yield a ranked score of candidates along with chemical structures with linked database, exact mass, neutral formula, and matched peaks. In this case, the query subject (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.0241) was annotated as GA (score of 1.0), with 4/7 peaks matching those of in silico-generated fragments of pure GA (1) deposited in the KEGG database. Raw data of twenty-four metabolites are shown in the <a href="#app1-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link">Supplementary Section (Table S1)</a>. Since our query subjects originated from a plant species, biological databases such as NORMAN, KEGG, and LipidMaps were chosen to evaluate the rate of correct identification (accuracy) of the natural products.</p></section><section id="sec2dot7-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.7. Structural Elucidation Using SIRIUS</h3>
<p>Raw mass data target metabolites (18 out of 24) were further elucidated with SIRIUS (version 5.8.3). Using the putative GA (1) as an example, its raw MS<sup>2</sup> data were first imported into the software. The MS<sup>2</sup> level with a CID value of 35 eV was defined, the precursor ion (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.0156) and the adduct type was set, where an expected neutral formula (i.e., C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>6</sub>O<sub>5</sub>) could be optionally guided for a more specific formula annotation of this query subject. SIRIUS, CSI:FingerID, and CANOPUS were all selected to acquire full details, where all biological databases (e.g., KEGG, NORMAN, Plantcyc, and Natural Products) were also chosen to improve the correctness of annotation of the inquiry metabolite. By doing so, the ion <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.0156 [M-H]<sup>−</sup> was best annotated as “gallic acid” in multiple aspects of elemental, structural, substructural, and class annotations, consolidating those predicted by an in-house MS/HRMS library and MetFrag.</p></section><section id="sec2dot8-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.8. Anti-Influenza Virus Screening Assay</h3>
<p>MDCK cells were seeded at 1.5 × 10<sup>5</sup> cells/well in a 96-well plate in a serum-free OptiMEM (Invitrogen, Waltham, MA, USA) supplemented with 2 µg/mL TPCK-treated trypsin and incubated at 5% CO<sub>2</sub> for 24 h at 37 °C. The influenza virus strain A/Puerto Rico/8/34 (H1N1) was diluted and mixed 1:1 with the extract for a final virus concentration of 100 pfu/well and a final extract concentration ranging from 1.56 to 200 μg/mL. After the cells were washed once with PBS, the virus-compound mixture was added and then incubated for an additional 24 h. Treated cells were then washed once with PBS, fixed with ice-cold acetone, and incubated at room temperature for 15 min. After washing with PBS, the cells were blocked with PBS containing 0.5% Tween 20 (PBST) and 2% BSA and incubated at room temperature for 30 min. Consequently, a 1:2000 dilution of the primary mouse anti-influenza A NP-UNLB antibody (BioLegend, San Diego, CA, USA) in PBST with 1% BSA was added to each well and incubated further for 60 min. Reactions were washed four times with PBS, followed by the addition of a 1:10,000 dilution of the secondary HRP-conjugated goat anti-mouse antibody (BioLegend) in PBST with 1% BSA and incubated for 60 min at room temperature. Signal detection was carried out by adding TMB substrate followed by incubation at room temperature for 8 min, and the signal was quantified with a microplate reader at an optical density of 450 nm. The toxicity of the extract towards MDCK cells was evaluated using the standard MTT assay as previously described [<a href="#B47-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-foods-13-00081">47</a>] at concentrations ranging from 100 to 0.05 μg/mL.</p></section><section id="sec2dot9-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.9. Hemolytic Activity Assay</h3>
<p>The hemolytic assay was established based on the hemolysis of human red blood cells (hRBCs) [<a href="#B48-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-foods-13-00081">48</a>]. Extract concentrations ranged from 800 to 100 µg/mL. In brief, hRBCs were resuspended in PBS (pH 7.4) at a 4% final concentration. One hundred microliters of suspension was transferred to a new microcentrifuge tube, followed by adding 10 µL of plant extract, and the mixture was incubated at 37 °C for 60 min. After that, reactions were centrifuged at 10,000× <em>g</em> for 5 min, and the obtained supernatant was transferred into a 96-well plate and measured at 415 nm. The hemolytic activity of plant samples was calculated as follows: % hemolysis = (S/P) × 100, where S represented the absorbance of samples (e.g., extract and PBS), while P indicated the absorbance of 0.1% Triton X-100 as a positive control. Under 15% hemolytic activity signifies that the plant extract is likely safe and could possibly be applicable for human use [<a href="#B49-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-foods-13-00081">49</a>].</p></section><section id="sec2dot10-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.10. Molecular Docking</h3>
<p>The crystal structures of influenza A (H1N1) HA (ID: 1RU7; resolution 2.30 Å) and NA (ID: 6HP0; resolution: 1.88 Å) were retrieved from the protein data bank (PDB) as representative structures of these viral proteins. To find the putative binding site, cavity detection was accomplished using CB-Dock (accessed on 12 September 2023 at <a href="https://cadd.labshare.cn/cb-dock2/php/index.php" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://cadd.labshare.cn/cb-dock2/php/index.php</a>), with oseltamivir acting as the query ligand. Of five candidates arranged by the binding scores, the second-rank (Vina Score of −5.4 kcal/mol) was chosen because of the specificity of the inquiry ligand towards the active site of the viral protein (Asn60, Ala62, Glu85, Ser89, Glu90, Ile93, Asp98, Phe99, Tyr102, Glu103, and Arg106) (<a href="#app1-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link">Figure S1</a>). Based on the basic binding of sialic acid (CID: 906), GJT (CID: 139030257), laninamivir (CID: 502272), peramivir (CID: 154234), oseltamivir (CID: 65028), zanamivir (CID: 60855), and DANA (CID: 445533) that have been found to inhibit the influenza virus via interacting with the catalytic site of NA were used as the control ligands. The GOLD 5.2.2 (Genetic Optimization of Ligand Docking) [<a href="#B15-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-foods-13-00081">15</a>], incorporated installed on Intel<sup>®</sup> Xeon<sup>®</sup> CPU2.30 GHz, 32 CPU, Core 8 Dell server with Linux operating systems, was used for molecular docking studies following the user guide [<a href="#B50-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-foods-13-00081">50</a>]. Self-docking for both HA/oseltamivir and NA/GJT complexes was conducted to ensure that their RMSD value is less than 2.0 Å; otherwise, the obtained results could be unacceptable. As predicted by the GOLD, the HA and NA received the RMSD of 1.7667 Å and 0.6478 Å, respectively, indicating both models are valid and could be openly reproduced to the inquiry metabolites from the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract. The generic algorithm parameters of GOLD were default settings, where the target ligands were docked separately to investigate whether they could interact with the active sites of viral surface proteins as the control ligands, e.g., sialic acid and anti-neuraminidase agents. The fitness score (assigned by GOLD) defines the negative of the sum of the component energy, where the larger fitness scores are better. CB-DOCK, a web tool designed for cavity detection, protein–ligand blind docking, and homologous template fitting along with the negative Gibbs free energy assignment (kcal/mol) through the “AutoDock Vena” in the docking process (<a href="https://cadd.labshare.cn/cb-dock2/php/blinddock.php" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://cadd.labshare.cn/cb-dock2/php/blinddock.php</a>, accessed on 6 August 2023). This tool was employed to validate the acquired outcomes by uploading a part of the protein–ligand complex (in pdb format originating from GOLD docking), ensuring their complete correspondence. Hence, the receptor–ligand complexes that were subsequently generated by GOLD and CB-DOCK (version 2) were used to explain the plausible mechanisms contributing to the anti-influenza properties of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract throughout this study.</p></section></section><section id="sec3-foods-13-00081"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">3. Results</h2>
<section id="sec3dot1-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.1. Radical Scavenging Activities of the C. mimosoides Aqueous Extract</h3>
<p>As mentioned, the ability to inhibit viral infection, especially in the case of water and ethanol extracts, is relatively straightforward for phenolic substances present in those extracts, where hydroxy groups belonging to aromatic ring systems interact with the active regions of viral surface proteins, including NA and HA [<a href="#B12-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-foods-13-00081">12</a>,<a href="#B13-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-foods-13-00081">13</a>]. Likewise, radical scavenging is involved in preventing cellular damage (e.g., oxidative stress) that occurs during viral entry or subsequent infection [<a href="#B51-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-foods-13-00081">51</a>,<a href="#B52-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-foods-13-00081">52</a>]. Thus, measuring antioxidant properties in parallel with defining the phenolic contents of extracts is a prerequisite. With the DPPH assay, the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract exhibited potent radical scavenging activity in a concentration-dependent manner with a minimum IC<sub>50</sub> value of 12.05 µg/mL, approximately 4.34-fold higher than that seen for GA (1) (2.78 µg/mL) (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>A). It also scavenged the ABTS radical with IC<sub>50</sub> values of 59.11 µg/mL while the GA (1) IC<sub>50</sub> was 29.41 µg/mL, a 2.01-fold difference in a dose-dependent manner (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>B). Notably, the obtained results provide a significant link between phenolic content (TPC) (551 mg (GAE)/g DE) (<a href="#foods-13-00081-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>) and the plant extract’s ability to scavenge free radicals (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>). This confirms our initial hypothesis that the aqueous extract of <em>C. mimosoides</em> is a viable source of phenolics with strong antioxidant properties.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f002"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/foods-13-00081-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section></section><section id="sec3dot2-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.2. Determination of GA (1) Using High-Performance Liquid Chromatography</h3>
<p>Previous studies conducted by our group showed that GA (1) was the major constituent presented in various extracts (e.g., <em>n</em>-hexane, MeOH, and EtOAc) of <em>C. mimosoides</em> [<a href="#B27-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-foods-13-00081">27</a>]. Thus, this compound was preliminarily chosen as the chemical marker for quality and quantity assessments of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> water extract using HPLC analysis. As shown in <a href="#foods-13-00081-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>, RP-HPLC analysis detected a significant peak (93.268% relative peak area) in the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract, whose retention time corresponded to authentic GA (1). Also, there was a smaller peak (31.594% relative peak area) eluted at the retention time of GA (1) found in the ethanolic extract of this plant species. No metabolites corresponding with GA (1) were detected in the blank samples (H<sub>2</sub>O and EtOH), indicating that contamination did not occur during the sample preparation phase. To verify these results, we spiked the GA (1) standard into the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract, which yielded only a single peak with a retention time of 10.081 min, suggesting that the major metabolite is likely to be GA (1) rather than any other phenolic substance. Quantitative analysis relying on the six-point calibration curve of GA (1) (15.625–500 μg/mL) with y = 75.25X + 101.82 (R<sup>2</sup> = 1) indicated the presence of 388.05 μg/mL of GA (1) in the aqueous extract, which is equal to 3.38 mg/g DW ground plant material. The yield of GA (1) in the ethanolic extract was 0.42 mg/g DW, approximately 8.03-fold lower than that detected in the aqueous extract. Therefore, the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract was chosen for untargeted metabolomics analysis to annotate the putative GA (1) peak and to characterize other phenolic substances that presumably govern its antioxidant and antiviral properties.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f003"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/689a2a99d4c6/foods-13-00081-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="471" width="737" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/foods-13-00081-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Qualitative and quantitative analysis of GA using HPLC. (<strong>A</strong>) Comparison of GA from different plant extracts. (<strong>B</strong>) Multi-point calibration curve of authentic gallic acid. The different colors represented the different samples analysed by HPLC analysis; meanwhlie, the color arrow to allocate the peaks that correponded to gallic acid, and to provide a linear correlation of the standard gallic acid.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3dot3-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.3. Metabolic Profiling of the Aqueous Extract of the C. mimosoides Using UPLC-ESI(-)-QTOF-MS/MS</h3>
<p>Numerous studies have indicated that the LC-MS/MS in negative ion mode analysis typically provides much more sensitivity in creating the negative-ion species of phenolic substances present in various plant extracts rather than in positive ion mode [<a href="#B53-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-foods-13-00081">53</a>,<a href="#B54-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-foods-13-00081">54</a>,<a href="#B55-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-foods-13-00081">55</a>]. Thus, high-resolution UPLC-ESI(-)-QTOF-MS/MS was performed, and detecting twenty-four hydroxy-containing metabolites in the <em>C. mimosoides</em> extract tentatively classified as phenolics, dicarboxylic acids, and sugar derivatives (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>). As summarized in <a href="#foods-13-00081-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>, their matching scores with those authentic spectra deposited with the in-house MS library ranged from 62.4% to 100%, with a mass error lower than 20 ppm, indicating that the obtained results were quite reliable. Remarkably, the putative GA (1) (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.0241 [M-H]<sup>−</sup>, Rt = 2.92 min) was detected as the major product among other substances detected (% peak area of 54.06), and its experimental fragmentation pattern was in line with the authentic GA (1) deposited in the PubChem database (accessed on 1 July 2023 at <a href="https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/compound/370" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://pubchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/compound/370</a>). This demonstrated that <em>C. mimosoides</em> could be prepared as a “GA-rich extract”, significantly reducing the number of organic solvents consumed.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f004"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/980928f98e8e/foods-13-00081-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="166" width="744" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn2"><p><sup>1</sup> Calculated from entire metabolites present in the sample plant extract. <sup>2</sup> Calculated from 24 metabolites possessed the library score reading 62.4–100%. <sup>3</sup> Based on information annotated by MetFrag.</p></div></div></section></section><section id="sec3dot4-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.4. Structural Annotation Using MetFrag</h3>
<p>Twenty-four substances were further characterized using MetFrag. As shown in <a href="#foods-13-00081-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>, 18 out of 24 query subjects could be structurally annotated as found in the MS library but ranked differently depending on the chosen databases. Using the ion <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 191.0607 as an example, among 702 candidates retrieved from the PubChem database, this query subject was annotated as quinic acid (126th) with a score of 1.0 and 4/7 peaks explained, implying additional annotation using distinguished databases was required. This tool also provides various biological databases to improve structural annotation of natural products. After comparison against KEGG and NORMAN, quinic acid rose to become one of the top-ranked candidates (1st and 2nd). This inquiry subject was thus presumably quinic acid rather than its related analogs possessing an identical molecular formula (C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>12</sub>O<sub>6</sub>). A similar trend was found in the case of putative azelaic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 187.0982). Its rank increased from 531st (PubChem) to first after analyzing against a lipid maps database. The six parent ions (369.0684, 193.0371, 165.0926, 455.2502, 315.2536, and 166.8340) that could not be annotated were excluded here. The remaining 18 metabolites were thus annotated using SIRIUS to their vital information regarding correct formula, structure, fingerprint, and class to fully support structural elucidation based on distinct cheminformatics approaches.</p>

<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="fn3"><p>* Structural annotation was based on the default values of the software. ** The candidate structures were selected based on their correspondence with the LC-MS-based annotation. N.D.: Not detected. N.A.: Not applicable.</p></div></div></section></section><section id="sec3dot5-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5. Structural Annotation Using SIRIUS</h3>
<p>SIRIUS is an in silico tool designed to annotate a correct molecular formula of inquiry subjects based on high-resolution isotope pattern analysis [<a href="#B38-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-foods-13-00081">38</a>]. This software is also integrated with CSI:FingerID and CANOPUS in order to provide structure and class, fingerprints (substructures), and classes of inquiry subjects from unknown mass spectra [<a href="#B37-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-foods-13-00081">37</a>,<a href="#B38-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-foods-13-00081">38</a>,<a href="#B39-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-foods-13-00081">39</a>]. After analysis using these tools, 17 out of 18 metabolites could be annotated to corresponding structures, as explained in <a href="#foods-13-00081-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>, <a href="#foods-13-00081-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>, <a href="#foods-13-00081-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a> and <a href="#foods-13-00081-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>. Their ranks as top candidates in terms of formula and structural annotation were remarkably high, indicating that reliable results were obtained. According to CANOPUS, they could be classified into four groups, the details of which are as follows.</p>
<section id="sec3dot5dot1-foods-13-00081"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.1. Simple Phenolic Substances</h4>
<p>Systematic compound classification revealed that seven metabolites in this group were solely synthesized via shikimate and phenylpropanoid pathways (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>). The use of high-resolution isotope pattern analysis led to unraveled-correct formulas of these phenolics, as observed from the highest SIRIUS scores (range 99.999–100%) among various formulas postulated using this tool. After searching against biological databases, CSI:FingerID confirmed that their structural information corresponded with those previously predicted by MetFrag and newly arranged as the first-ranked candidate (1st), indicating a higher reliability of the structural annotation. Several substructures (so-called “fingerprints”) that represent aromatic core structures (C<sub>6</sub>-C<sub>1</sub>), hydroxy groups, and carboxylic groups were also predicted to be present in these compounds, further supporting the precision of the predictions. The query subject at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.0156 [M-H]<sup>−</sup>, for example, out of the ten formulas retrieved, was best annotated as C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>6</sub>O<sub>5</sub> (SIRIUS score: 100.000%), with six from seven peaks explained, which is higher than that of the MetFrag web service (4/7) (<a href="#foods-13-00081-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>). When an expected molecular formula (C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>6</sub>O<sub>5</sub>) was guided during the data-filling process, the SIRIUS score reached 100%. CSI:FingerID also showed that this query metabolite (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 169.0156; C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>6</sub>O<sub>5</sub>) was annotated as GA (100% similarity; CSI score: −15.837) from among the one hundred candidates retrieved from numerous databases (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>F). Several substructures of GA were detected in this query subject. For instance, fingerprint encoded by “[#8]=,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]- ,:[#6]-, :[#6]-, :[#6]- ,:[#8] (O=C-C-C-C-C-O)” indicated intercorrelation between carbonyl group (C=O) and the hydroxy group (-OH) located on the ring system. Two substructures, encoded by “[#8][#6]1[#6][#6]([#8][#6]1 [#6][#6]1(OC1CC(O)CCC1)” and “[#8][#6]1[#6][#8][#6][#6][#6][#6]1(OC1C(O)CC CC1)”, represented the covalent linkage between the benzene ring and two oxygen atoms, while a hydroxy group (O;H0) was also deduced in the same query subject. Since pyrogallols and derivatives are alternative classes of GA, successive annotation of the six remaining metabolites was also a consequence since they share identical synthetic routes in many plant species [<a href="#B56-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-foods-13-00081">56</a>,<a href="#B57-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-foods-13-00081">57</a>]. This reliability of annotation might be supported by the presence of authentic mass spectra of these phenolics in CSI: FingerID training data (accessed on 20 July 2023 at <a href="https://www.csi-fingerid.uni-jena.de/v2.6/api/fingerid/trainingstructures?predictor=2" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.csi-fingerid.uni-jena.de/v2.6/api/fingerid/trainingstructures?predictor=2</a>). Benzoic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 121.0663) is a notable example, where it entirely shifted to first since its data were deposited in the training database (InChI=1S/C<sub>7</sub>H<sub>6</sub>O<sub>2</sub>/c8-7(9)6-4-2-1-3-5-6/h1-5H,(H,8,9)).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f005"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h5>

<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g005c.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/d5f342881361/foods-13-00081-g005c.jpg" loading="lazy" height="1017" width="783" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/foods-13-00081-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Tentative structural identification of simple phenolic acids found in the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract using SIRIUS, CSI: FingerID, and CANOPUS. (<strong>A</strong>) Salicylic acid; (<strong>B</strong>) (-)-Quinic acid; (<strong>C</strong>) Shikimic acid; (<strong>D</strong>) Protocatechuic acid; (<strong>E</strong>) Pyrogallol; (<strong>F</strong>) Gallic acid; (<strong>G</strong>) Benzoic acid. All of the query subjects (<strong>A</strong>–<strong>G</strong>) were predicted to receive the correct molecular formula, based on the matched mass spectra (indicated in “green”), as the first-rank candidate with the highest SIRIUS score (87.13% to 100%). The matched tandem mass spectra (green color) were then translated into potential structures using CSI:FingerID web service, based on the pairwise comparison with those present in the biological databases (e.g., KEGG, NORMAN, and Natural Products). By taking molecular fingerprints defined by CSI:FingerID web service, CANOPUS then predicted they all share the same shikimic route and phenolic acid (C<sub>6</sub>-C<sub>1</sub>) is the superclass of the natural products.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3dot5dot2-foods-13-00081"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.2. Methylated Analogs</h4>
<p>O-Methylation is crucial for an increasing number of biological activities of natural products, where transferring a methyl moiety onto acceptor molecules (e.g., simple phenolic) yields methylated analogs possessing great structural diversity with improved lipophilic properties [<a href="#B58-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-foods-13-00081">58</a>]. Four putative methylated phenolic derivatives, including ions at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 135.0822, 327.0743, 151.0402, and 193.0881, were found in the aqueous plant extract (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>). With the use of high-resolution isotope pattern analysis, the expected elemental formula of these methylated metabolites was postulated according to the highest scores (ranging from 99.618 to 100%). All metabolites except an ion with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 193.0881 were structurally annotated as the top-ranked candidates consistent with MetFrag web service, signifying much more reliable results. Substructural annotation also revealed that the putative 3-methoxybenzoic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 151.0402; Rt = 4.13 min) shares numerous features with its parent benzoic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 121.0663; Rt = 4.40 min). For example, the substructure encoded as “[#8]=,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6](O=C-C-C-C-C)”, whose SIRIUS score of 97.901% (F1 = 0.923), illustrating positive correlation between the four carbon atoms and the carbonyl group (C=O) located in the benzene ring. They differed from each other, however, due to the presence or absence of a methyl group (-CH<sub>3</sub>) ether bonded (R-O-R’) with a hydroxy group on the basic ring system (C6-C1). These included C;H3,H4, cOC(39alkylaryl ether), c(:c(:c:c:c:1)~[#8]):c:1, [!#1]O[CH3], COc1ccccc1, Cc1cccc(O)c1, and [OX2](c) [CX4;!$(C([OX2])[O,S,#7,#15,F,Cl,Br,I])] (Alkylarylether). These results became more rigid by the detection of a product ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 136.0176, which illustrates an entire loss of a methyl group (~15.0238 Da) from its core structure (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> = 151.0402). The resulting product is subject to consecutive loss of the carboxy group (CO<sub>2</sub>: 43.9886 Da), yielding the second-most abundant peak at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 92.0275 (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>C). Putative bergenin <em>(m</em>/<em>z</em> 327.0743; Rt = 3.69 min) also consisted of fingerprints that illustrate successive C-glycosylation and O-methylation of GA, which could be characterized by cOC (39 al-kyl aryl ether),“[CH1]([CH1] [OH0][CH1][CH1]-1~[#8])([CH1]-1~[#8])~[#8], and [CH1](~[!#1])[CH1]([CH1]([CH1]( [CH1]( CH<sub>2</sub>]~[!#1])~O)~O)~O)~[!#1]. Also, substructures showing a successive pyrone ring formation between GA and a sugar moiety were detected in the same structural candidate, which could be described by [#6]~C(c(:c:c:c:c1~[131]):c1)~[!#1],Cc1ccc(O)cc1)~[!#1]):c1 [CH0](~[#1])~[!#1] and Cc1ccc(O)cc1. Compound class annotation also supported 2-benzopyrans, isocoumarins, and coumarins as alternative classes of this inquiry subject. Unfortunately, the negative ion with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 193.0881, formerly annotated as 3-hydroxy-4-methoxy cinnamic acid (<a href="#foods-13-00081-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>), could not be unraveled by CSI:FingerID due to its limited fragmentation spectra. Although it contains the product ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 178.064, suggesting a methyl loss (CH<sub>3</sub>: 15.0238) from the cinnamic core structure, optimizing collision-induced energy is still needed to fully support the tentative identification of this methylated analog, and it was hence ruled out from this study.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f006"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g006a.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/68232f073230/foods-13-00081-g006a.jpg" loading="lazy" height="657" width="753" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g006b.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/892bdf062355/foods-13-00081-g006b.jpg" loading="lazy" height="324" width="747" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/foods-13-00081-f006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Structural annotation of putative methylated analogs synthesized via shikimate and phenylpropanoid pathway. (<strong>A</strong>) Bergenin; (<strong>B</strong>) 4-Hydroxyacetophenone; (<strong>C</strong>) 3-Methoxybenzoic acid. The high-resolution isotope pattern analysis illustrates the correct elemental formula of the three methylated derivatives (<strong>A</strong>–<strong>C</strong>) detected in the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract (<strong>A</strong>–<strong>C</strong>). The matched tandem mass spectra (green color) of each metabolite were then subjected to structural annotation using CSI:FingerID web service. Numerous molecular fingerprints showing the presence of specific <em>O</em>-methylation aromatic core structures were also assigned by the CSI:FingerID web service with the highest posterior probability score (F1). CANOPUS clearly revealed that they all originated from the shikimate pathway, where phenolic acid (C<sub>6</sub>-C<sub>1</sub>) serves as the “Superclass” of these methylated derivatives.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3dot5dot3-foods-13-00081"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.3. Sugar Derivatives</h4>
<p>Two putative peaks that corresponded with sorbitol (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 181.073; C<sub>6</sub>H<sub>14</sub>O<sub>6</sub>) and D-arabinonic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 165.0414; C<sub>5</sub>H<sub>10</sub>O<sub>6</sub>) were best explained by SIRIUS and the CSI:FingerID web service. Sorbitol is a reduced product of glucose that is catalyzed by aldose reductases. Substructural elucidation revealed substructures that indicate a successive reduction of glucose to the sugar alcohol detected in the first query subject. For example, fingerprints encoded by “[#8]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#8] (O-C-C-C-O)”, “[#8]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#8] (O-C-C-C-C-O)”, and “[#8]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6],-,:[#6]-,[#6] (O-C-C-C-C-C-C)” clearly illustrate a correlation between various carbon chain lengths (C<sub>3</sub>-C<sub>5</sub>) and oxygen atoms at the termini. This is consolidated by the presence of hydroxyl group, denoted by “[OX2H][CX4;!$(C([OX2H])[O, S,#7,#15])](Alcohol)” and “[O; H0]” in this structural potential. Although polyols and primary alcohols are alternative classes of this sugar derivative, D-mannitol might also be a potential candidate due to a highly identical fragmentation profiling deposited in the PubChem database (accessed on 20 July 2023 at <a href="https://puchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/#query=D-mannitol" class="usa-link" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://puchem.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/#query=D-mannitol</a>). Since both sugar alcohols are isomeric structures sharing polyol biosynthetic routes in numerous plant species [<a href="#B59-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-foods-13-00081">59</a>], further elucidation is needed to support the computational-based annotation. Putative D-arabinonic acid also shares identical features to that of sorbitol, such as “[#8] -,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#8] (O-C-C-C-C-O)” and “[O; H0]”. Even so, they could be distinguished from one another based on particular substructures encoded by “[#8]=,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6] (O=C-C-C-C)” and “[#8]=,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6] (O=C-C-C-)”, which show the presence of carboxylic group, with hydroxy acids and derivatives as ancestors of this sugar derivative.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f007"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/1b67363c6d5b/foods-13-00081-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="654" width="755" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/foods-13-00081-f007/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Formula, structure, and substructure annotation of sugar derivatives detected from the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract. (<strong>A</strong>) D-Arabinonic acid; (<strong>B</strong>) D-Sorbitol. Isotope pattern analysis decodes the correct molecular formula of putative sugar derivatives (<strong>A</strong>,<strong>B</strong>) from the matched MS<sup>2</sup> spectrum (showing in green color) with a SIRIUS score of 100%. The matched tandem mass spectra of each metabolite were then subjects for structural annotation using CSI:FingerID web service, where the negative ions with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 165.0414 and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 181.073 were best annotated as D-Arabinonic acid and D-Sorbitol, respectively. By taking the relevant substructures (molecular fingerprints) predicted by CSI:FingrerID web service, CANOPUS reveals that both sugars are categorized in class and subclass of saccharides, wherein “Organic compound” is a kingdom of these two natural products.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3dot5dot4-foods-13-00081"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.5.4. Dicarboxylic Acids</h4>
<p>Short and medium-chain fatty acids (C<sub>4</sub>, C<sub>9</sub>-C<sub>16</sub>) were also detected and predicted to originate from fatty acid biosynthetic pathways. These included negative precursor ions with <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 133.0149, 187.0982, 227.2021, 257.1776, and 285.2081, respectively (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>). CSI: FingerID provides substructural details hidden in the query subjects. As an example, the ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 187.0892 [M-H]<sup>−</sup> was annotated as “azelaic acid” as the first example. As depicted by “CCCC=O”,“CCC(=O)C”, “[#6][CX3](C=O)”, and ‘’[#8]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,:[#6]-,[#6]-,:[#6] (O-C-C-C-C-C), these fingerprints imply the connection between aliphatic hydrocarbons (C<sub>3</sub>-C<sub>5</sub>) and a carboxyl group. A positive correlation between hydrocarbons and carboxylic group, encoded by “[!#1][CH<sub>2</sub>]C(=O)[OH]” and [#6](-,:[#6])(-,:[#8])(=,:[#8] (C(-C)(-O)(=O)), was also present in the same query subject. The putative <em>trans</em>-Traumatic acid (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 227.2021) is distinguished from other class members via exclusive detection of the molecular fingerprint “[#6]=,:[#6] (C=C)” signifying a double bond (C=C) formation in this inquiry subject. This substructure was clearly absent in other dicarboxylic acids considering the lowest posterior probability scores (0.351–3.544%), suggesting structural annotation of the product ion at <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 227.2021 could possibly be reliable.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f008"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g008a.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/f211f2825d8b/foods-13-00081-g008a.jpg" loading="lazy" height="977" width="777" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g008b.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/a11f0a3eff6a/foods-13-00081-g008b.jpg" loading="lazy" height="665" width="763" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/foods-13-00081-f008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Molecular formula, structure, and substructure annotation of dicarboxylic acids detected from <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract. (<strong>A</strong>) DL-malic acid; (<strong>B</strong>) Azelaic acid; (<strong>C</strong>) <em>trans</em>-Traumatic acid; (<strong>D</strong>) Hexadecanedioic acid; (<strong>E</strong>) Tetradecanedioic acid. The high-resolution isotope pattern analysis unveils the correct molecular formula of putative dicarboxylic acids (<strong>A</strong>–<strong>E</strong>) that are hidden in the matched MS2 spectrum (as indicated in green) with a SIRIUS score of 100%. The green spectra of each metabolite were then translated into their highly corresponded chemical structure using CSI:FingerID web service, where numerous substructures (so-called fingerprints) that represent being of linear chain hydrocarbons and dicarboxylic acids are also detected in the query metabolites. CANOPUS, a compound classification tool, verifies that fatty acids, fatty acyls, and organic compounds are class, superclass, and kingdom of all-natural products, respectively.</p></figcaption></figure></section></section><section id="sec3dot6-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.6. Antiviral and Hemolytic Activity of the C. mimosoides Aqueous Extract</h3>
<p>We assessed the antiviral activity of the extract by testing it against an influenza A H1N1 virus. Cytotoxicity assays against MDCK cells revealed a cytotoxicity concentration (CC<sub>50</sub>) greater than 100 µg/mL and identified a range of extract concentrations that proved non-toxic. Extracts were diluted within this range and pre-mixed with the virus prior to addition to MDCK cells for infection. The virus-compound mixture was left on the cells for 24 h, enabling assessment of antiviral activity throughout the course of virus infection and replication. Results showed that our plant extract was capable of inhibiting virus infection with an IC<sub>50</sub> of 5.14 µg/mL (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>B). Given that the compound was present from the attachment through replication and budding stages, inhibition may have occurred at any stage of the viral life cycle. The difference between IC<sub>50</sub> and CC<sub>50</sub> values demonstrates that the extract can be used at an effective antiviral concentration without detrimental effects on the host cells. As GA (1) is the main component of the extract, pure GA was also tested and found to inhibit H1N1 infection with an IC<sub>50</sub> of 4.35 µg/mL and a CC<sub>50</sub> higher than 100 µg/mL (<a href="#foods-13-00081-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>B,C).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="foods-13-00081-f009"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=10778375_foods-13-00081-g009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/1ac1/10778375/e5ab2666f78d/foods-13-00081-g009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="452" width="748" alt="Figure 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/foods-13-00081-f009/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Hemolytic and antiviral activities of <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract and gallic acid. (<strong>A</strong>) Hemolytic activity assay; (<strong>B</strong>) IC<sub>50</sub> defines the concentration of a substance or an extract that is required to inhibit viral replication by 50%, where the lower IC<sub>50</sub> values signify a greater antiviral potency; (<strong>C</strong>) CC<sub>50</sub> is used in the framework of cytotoxicity assays, meaning the concentration of a plant extract and/or the authentic GA (1) that cause a 50% reduction in the viability of the MDCK cells. In this case, the IC<sub>50</sub> value for the <em>C. mimosoides</em> extract is remarkably lower than the CC<sub>50</sub> value, implying its effective antiviral concentration without inducing harmful effects on the host cells.</p></figcaption></figure><p>While <em>C. mimosoides</em> is consumed regularly and has long been used as traditional medicine, its aqueous extract was tested with a hemolytic assay to ensure low toxicity toward human red blood cells (hRBCs), which can be considered a surrogate for normal cells. As shown in <a href="#foods-13-00081-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>A, even at the highest concentration tested (800 µg/mL), the plant extract did not show hemolytic activity, unlike the positive control Triton X-100. This indicates the potential applicability of <em>C. mimosoides</em> water extracts as an alternative and safe antiviral agent.</p></section><section id="sec3dot7-foods-13-00081"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7. Molecular Docking for Anti-Influenza Activity</h3>
<section id="sec3dot7dot1-foods-13-00081"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">3.7.1. Target Specificity towards Viral Surface Proteins</h4>
<p>Molecular docking was implemented to explain mechanisms underlying the antiviral activity of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract. As mentioned, hemagglutinin (HA) and neuraminidase (NA) play a significant role in the process of viral entry and release of influenza viruses [<a href="#B12-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-foods-13-00081">12</a>,<a href="#B13-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-foods-13-00081">13</a>,<a href="#B14-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-foods-13-00081">14</a>,<a href="#B15-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-foods-13-00081">15</a>,<a href="#B16-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-foods-13-00081">16</a>,<a href="#B17-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-foods-13-00081">17</a>,<a href="#B60-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-foods-13-00081">60</a>]. They have thus been promising candidates for developing targeted inhibitors to block critical steps of the viral cycle [<a href="#B60-foods-13-00081" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-foods-13-00081">60</a>]. Molecular docking studies revealed that all of the complexities were predicted to have negative Gibbs free energies (ranging from −3.2 kcal/mol to −7.2 kcal/mol), indicating the reactions can proceed and are in accordance with the second law of thermodynamics (<a href="#foods-13-00081-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>). The results showed that the target ligands preferentially interact with neuraminidase (NA: 6HP0) over hemagglutinin (HA: 1RU7), indicating that the former protein (NA) is the feasible target inhibitor for all metabolites detected in this study and should be further explored into the details to unravel a plausible mechanism of actions contributing the antiviral activity of the <em>C. mimosoides</em> aqueous extract.</p>
